| 2 | 2 | 5 | The Modern School piled up a big score against Northants. Amateurs on the School field on Wednesday, declaring with 215 for 5 wickets. The main contributors were K. C. Campin 50 not out, D. R. Goodwin (40), G. Harrison (38), and D. E. Farr (37). The Amateurs failed, with the exception of C. J. Longland, who opened the innings and scored 104 not out. When stumps were drawn they had scored 154 for 7, and the game was left drawn. Scores:- |

| 3 | 2 | 6 | Monday and Tuesday, v. Oxfordshire: R. de W. K. Winlaw, A. B. Poole, G. L. B. August, J. A. R. Oliver, E. Payne, N. E. Browning, A. Haywood, J. Payne, J. G. Owen, D. John, and E. D. Markham; twelfth man, D. B. Coleman. |
| 3 | 13 | 21 | The Bedford Martlets Cricket Club left Bedford this week for their fifth annual tour of Kent. The seventeen members who make up the party have their headquarters at East Farleigh, Maidstone, and the teams to be played are in a thirty-five mile radius of this place. The tour opened with a match against the Royal Marines, Chatham (yesterday), and there is a match on each day (excepting Sunday) until 12th August. The other fixtures will be against Tunbridge Wells, Barming, Whitstable and Tankerton, Frindsbury, Gravesend, Tonbridge, Hythe, and Cranbrook. The touring team is: E. J. Cooper (captain), W. Moore (Cheltenham), E. S. Volz, F. Marshall, D. T. Stevens, H. Steele-Smith (Selwyn College), N. R. Larby (O.B.M.), P. H. Thomas (Selwyn College), K. J. Petts (Welwyn), D. C. H. Wright (Southgate), W. M. E. White (the Army), R. Chatterton (Mill Hill School), V. A. Gubbins (Southgate), N. E. Gillham (Southgate), B. C. Barnwell (O.B.M.), K. C. Campin (Modern School), and D. R. Goodwin (Modern School). In 1938 nine matches were played, of which seven were won by the Martlets. |
| 3 | 15 | 23 | It will be interesting to see how the young Modern School medium-fast bowler, Campin, fares on the tour. He was outstanding in the School games and captured forty-five wickets—a record number—out of the seventy-six wickets taken. Of 171 overs he sent down forty-three were maidens, and the average cost of each wicket was only 7.8. His figures prove his consistency: v. King Edward School, seven for 51 (thirty-one overs); v. Royal Masonic, five for 37; v. Emmanuel, four for 35; v. Wellingborough, five for 23; v. Oakham, five for 19; v. Reading, eight for 49; v. Forty Club, seven for 86; v. Northamptonshire Amateurs, four for 54. Total, forty-five for 354; average, 7.8. |
| 4 | 2 | 3 | Old Bedfordian Week has come and gone once again, and its enjoyable social events and sporting encounters pass into pleasant memory. The Old Boys beat the boys at cricket—an exciting victory—were beaten by the boys and by the masters at tennis, and had their usual Sunday golf match with the Bedfordshire Club. There were also swimming and rowing contests with the School. On Sunday the annual meeting of the Old Bedfordians Club was held, and the Head Master gave a garden party, and on Friday there was an “At Home” followed by the Old Bedfordians’ Ball, which is reported on another page. ⏎ This season’s Bedford School cricket ended on a high note with the two-day twelve-a-side match against the Old Bedfordians. Although forced to follow on, the School struck back to such effect in their second innings that at 6.25 p.m. on Saturday Doubleday, the captain, was in a position to make a sporting declaration. He left the Old Bedfordians with seventy-seven to score in thirty-five minutes. The Old Bedfordians promptly accepted the challenge and sent out Sime and Oakley with instructions to get a move on. And Sime and Oakley did get a move on. They got the runs with some minutes to spare! |

| 4 | 17 | 109 | Sir,—The County Cricket Week begins next Monday and the playing prospects are definitely bright (weather permitting). The Eleven made an excellent start this season when they put up such a good performance at Luton against Sir Julien Cahn’s strong team. The match was drawn, the County leading on the first innings—fine all-round cricket, good batting, and steady, good-length bowling backed up by excellent fielding, which, perhaps, was the most pleasing feature of the game. In these days of plumb wickets it is essential, and only fair to the hard-working bowlers, that special attention should be paid, when selecting a team, to the important department of fielding. |

| 7 | 2 | 3 | After ruining outdoor sports engagements on Saturday, it looked as though rain was going to repeat its evil on Monday in the early part of the morning. The main sports fixture was the opening match of the County Cricket Week on the Bedford School playing field against Oxfordshire. The pitch was sodden after the torrential rain of Sunday, but by midday on Monday the weather brightened and a start was made on a newly-prepared crease to the left of the pavilion—away from the School—at about 1.40 p.m. The play that followed was in direct contrast to the weather, being bright and exhilarating to the Bedfordshire supporters. By winning by an innings and 89 runs Bedfordshire made a grand start to the Week. How they fared against Buckinghamshire will be seen by the report in another column. |
| 7 | 5 | 6 | Although it was on the board that the wicket might play tricks later on it was a surprise to see Oxfordshire wickets going down like ninepins in the very early stages. The wicket was as sodden as a pudding and the bowlers had a job to gain a foothold. Yet there were Payne and Oliver having a real bank holiday. Payne bowled Money with the fourth ball of the first over with only one run on the board, and without addition to the score and in the same over the footballer had Parton lbw. Not to be outdone, Oliver sent back Lithgow in his first over, August taking a catch in the slips. Williams, who followed, survived one ball and had his wicket broken with the next from Oliver. Four for two runs, one run being from a no-ball. |
| 7 | 8 | 9 | Anyhow Oxfordshire’s sorry plight went on. Two left-arm spin bowlers, Haywood and Owen, the latter bowling over the wicket, replaced the medium-fast attack of Payne and Oliver, and they, too, met with success, but not of such phenomenal order as the opening pair. The ball not turning fast enough to do justice to the wiles of the slow men. With eight wickets down for 28 Oxfordshire wanted a “do-or-die” run, and they had one in O’Shea. While Bowtell went on in a steady, patient way O’Shea tried an aggressive bat. He had all the luck in the world for some time. Owen tied him up in knots without being able to get his wicket. Oliver and Markham had spells with the ball, but it was Haywood who broke the partnership, which put on 56 for the ninth wicket. The Three Counties Hospital player “spun” Bowtell out. The ball almost “stood” before it turned and made the Oxford man give the easiest of catches to Markham. He had scored a valuable 22. O’Shea went on hitting out and compiled 42 not out. It was through him and Bowtell that his side’s total reached 85. |
| 7 | 10 | 11 | Payne had the best return of the home bowlers with three wickets for nine runs, Oliver having three for eighteen, and Haywood three for 23. These returns tell a story, but the fall of the wickets are more eloquent testimony of the way the game went—1 for 1, 2 for 1, 3 for 1, 4 for 2, 5 for 12, 6 for 12, 7 for 22, 8 for 28, 9 for 84, 10 for 85. |
| 7 | 11 | 12 | Bedfordshire made an exceedingly good start through August and Oliver. These two young players were steady and orthodox and were together until 64 had been scored. Oliver was the first to go for 28. Owen came in next and made his bow to the Bedford public as a batsman. The spectators liked him for his enterprise and fine—sometimes almost impudent—running between the wickets. Owen saw to it that the score was advanced at a rapid rate and made the imperturbable August scurry. August would play in a Test match without turning a hair, for he has that temperament. The Old Bedfordian reached his fifty, but added only one more run before he was out. He played back too late to a ball which turned sharply. The score was 99 for two. |
| 7 | 14 | 15 | Winlaw followed Payne and the scoring rate dropped. The Bedfordshire captain took no risks and in a streaky innings he had a life when short-leg dropped a catch right into his hands. Owen left at 136 and Poole came in. A. B. at once hit out, but it was some minutes before he broke his “duck”, for he could not get the ball through the well-placed field. Then Winlaw gave another catch to the man who had missed him, and this time Florey was not so generous and Winlaw was out for 12. Poole got going nicely and collected his runs at a rapid pace. He and John were together at the close of play when the total stood at 199 for five and Poole’s score at 39. The wicket was badly worn. |
| 7 | 17 | 18 | O’Shea came on with the new ball at Bowtell’s end. Runs came at a good pace. John being particularly strong on the on side, but Browning seemed a little uncertain when facing Bowtell, who has taken up the attack at the School end, and at 219 a leg-break hit the Bedford second master’s off peg. The soft wicket was obviously to Bowtell’s liking. Markham shaped stylishly for a few minutes, but with six runs added this young batter went forward to Bowtell and the stumps were spreadeagled. Haywood succumbed first ball. John and Page took the score to 233, at which total the innings closed. Five Bedfordshire wickets had gone in 83½ minutes for the addition of sixty runs. Bowtell’s figures on Tuesday were four wickets for 19 runs. All told he took six for 64. |
| 7 | 19 | 20 | Faced with the considerable task of getting 141 runs to avoid an innings defeat, Oxfordshire opened with Lithgow and Peto to the bowling of Payne (School) and Oliver. With the last ball of the first over Oliver induced Lithgow to step across and the batsman was out lbw, one run having been scored. Payne might have had Peto taken in the slips, and then with the score at two Oliver sent a fine ball through Peto’s defence. The total stood at three when the third wicket fell, a ball from Payne taking Parton’s bails. Frequently deliveries from both bowlers reared disconcertingly and Money and Bowtell were anything but easy at the Twice Bowtell almost played on to Payne. At long Oliver had Money caught at stand slip, the Luton bowler having taken three wickets for six runs in three overs and two balls. The first aggressive stroke of the innings was a four to leg by Florey off Payne. Florey decided that an attacking policy was the best in the circumstances and he took thirteen runs off an over by the Chelsea footballer, who was now bowling round the wicket. Florey went on collecting runs by attractive strokes, and he and Bowtell had ridden 27 when the latter was caught at the wicket. ⏎ All Over ⏎ Markham relieved Payne, and Williams was lbw to his second ball 37-6-3. A 49 Florey’s free-going venture came to an end; Haywood, who had gone on to Oliver, led him caught behind the wicket. He hit four boundaries. Page was in his best “appealing” form; at 54 the umpire’s finger went up in response to another inquiry from the diminutive stumper, and out went Kunn. Carr tried to hit Haywood for six and was bowled, the score being 59. Oliver came into the attack again and immediately took the last wicket. |
| 7 | 20 | 24 | Oliver’s exceptional bowling figures were: 6.2 overs, 2 maidens, 8 runs, 7 wickets. Haywood had three wickets for eight runs in three overs. Markham took one for 14, and Payne one for 28. |
| 8 | 1 | 4 | Henlow lost on Monday to Welwyn Garden City. Welwyn, batting first, were soon in difficulties, losing four wickets for 37. Then Bullock and Howard added 29 for the fifth wicket. After they left the side collapsed and were all out for 83—a good total considering the state of the wicket. It was too big a score for the home side, and with wickets tumbling regularly they could reach only 52. There were three outstanding features. Swain, for Henlow, in taking six for 12, captured his last four wickets with four consecutive balls. Lanning bowled well to return figures of seven for 13. The third feature was the wicketkeeping of Carter, of Welwyn. He stumped three and caught one. Other bowlers were L. Lynn (3 for 28) and J. Reast (3 for 18). Scores:— |

| 8 | 10 | 22 | Good bowling was the feature of Ampthill’s success when they entertained Wolverton Technical College team in the Park on Monday. | 8 | 14 | 33 | After a blank day on Saturday owing to the rain, Biggleswade entertained their neighbours, Caldecote, at Fairfield on Monday. A low-scoring game ended in a win for Caldecote on the first innings by 26 runs. Caldecote’s first innings score of 57 was mainly contributed by the captain, D. L. MacLean (26), and E. King (15). A. Giles bowled well and took six wickets for 13 runs. The home batsmen could do little against the good bowling of King and O’Dell, and were soon dismissed for only 31. |

| 9 | 11 | 10 | The Bedford Town second eleven scored an easy victory at Royston on Monday, defeating the home team by 130 runs to 36. P. H. Humphreys was mainly instrumental in dismissing the home side so cheaply, his six wickets costing him only seven runs. Sleightholm captured the remaining four wickets for 24 runs. |
| 9 | 12 | 11 | Bedford batted first and lost two quick wickets, Ferriman and Humphreys being out with only two runs on the board. Gompertz followed soon afterwards, but a stand by Howard and Ward added 41 runs for the fourth wicket. Howard hit five fours and two sixes, and Ward and Sleightholm also hit a six each. However, the last four batsmen added only 27 runs, and the innings closed at 180. ⏎ Sleightholm took a wicket in his second over, and after that wickets fell in quick succession and the Royston innings ended at 36. They fared better in a second innings, scoring 78 for four wickets. The scores were: ⏎ Bedford Town II: R. F. Ferriman b Palmer 0. C. H. Humphreys c Palmer b Sutton 0. P. J. Gompertz lbw b Sutton 12. | 10 | 23 | 39 | St. Neots were away to Stewartby on Saturday, a new fixture, and came away winners by 63 runs, after declaring. St. Neots did not start their innings well, losing the wickets of Owen and Bunnage with only 11 on the board. Craig and Jones batted well, however, and put on 47 for the third wicket before Jones was bowled after making a sound 24, his highest for St. Neots first eleven. After this, four St. Neots wickets fell quickly, but Sillett joined Craig, and this pair added 64 for the eighth wicket. Craig made 67 before being bowled. He batted with great steadiness. Sillett also made his highest score of the season and his 31 included some excellent drives. The St. Neots innings was declared closed at 154 for nine. |
| 10 | 24 | 40 | Stewartby made an excellent start in their reply, Tysoe and Hudson putting on 55 before being parted, but after the opening partnership was broken the rest of the batsmen collapsed and it was a question as to whether St. Neots would beat the clock and dismiss Stewartby before 7.30, the time fixed for the close of play. With two overs to go St. Neots had still to get rid of two remaining London Brick Company batsmen, but they managed to do it with three balls to spare. Craig, by taking five for 20, completed a good all-round performance for St. Neots. Scores:— |

| 11 | 2 | 2 | Martlets’ Tour of Kent Nearly Ruined |
| 11 | 3 | 3 | The Bedford Martlets’ annual cricket tour of Kent was this year nearly ruined by rain. It was not until the last two days that summer weather prevailed. The matches against the Royal Marines at Chatham, K.C.M.H., Barming, and Tonbridge were abandoned without a ball being bowled. The first game at Linden Park, Tunbridge Wells, was played on a wet, but drying wicket, and the Martlets decided to bat after some hesitation. Having practice on concrete at their headquarters the Martlets could not settle down and they were out for 50. Heavy rain threatened to render further play impossible, but a fresh wicket was cut for Linden Park’s innings. Off the first ball N. B. Larby made a catch of Campin (3 for 17) at short leg, and the home side lost three for 10 and four for 28. Rain began to fall again but Linden Park reached 53 for five. |
| 11 | 13 | 15 | Henlow were beaten by 60 runs at Letchworth on Saturday. Their batsmen, with the exception of R. Lynn and F. Thorogood, failed dismally. Lynn played a grand innings, making 32, including one glorious hit for six. He is regaining his form after his long period of enforced absence. Alexander bowled brilliantly to capture five for eighteen and Grey returned three for thirty. Henlow started off well, Russell bowling Diss first ball, but that was the extent of their success until their total had been passed. Grey and Taylor adding 92 in fifty minutes for the second wicket. Grey, the Bedford Rugby Club full-back, batted brilliantly for his 51, including six fours and a six. Taylor was more subdued in making 28. Fenton, (25) also batted well. A. R. Carlisle, the Henlow skipper, with his tantalizing slows, was their best bowler, taking six for 43. Russell took two for 26. Scores:- |

| 17 | 3 | 5 | Let us presume that all is well and look at the sides as though they are sure to turn out as selected. Will Bedford win at Leicester for the fourth year in succession? I think that they will, for their fifteen is better balanced than the “Tigers” team and they will go on the field with the knowledge of three victories to their credit in the last three years. Bedford will be led by Gilbert Cook and he will have a very strong combination to command. The team, which no follower can quarrel about, is as follows: J. E. I. Grey; T. D. Thevenard, J. G. Rogers, E. H. Walshe, and V. J. Lytle; M. E. Staples and R. Smyth; G. T. Danger, B. J. McMaster, G. A. Kelly, R. Wilsher, A. E. Cook, J. G. Cook, H. J. Irens, and O. V. Bevan. |
| 17 | 4 | 6 | Leicester have selected the following: Bar; Cooke, Squibbs, Edwards, and Pearce; Taylor and Gadney; Boldsworth, Ridgeway, Herbert, Jerwood, Doe, Anthony Richards, and Berry. J. T. W. Berry is again captain, and he will be glad to have in the side once more Bernard Gadney, who has thrown in his lot with Leicester after a short spell with Headingley, who will be Bedford’s visitors next week. Gadney still holds a teaching appointment at Leeds, however. Great hopes are held of Pearce, a sturdy winger, who, in the words of a Leicester colleague, “needs a lot of holding”. He is last year’s captain of the Uppingham fifteen. Our D. L. K. Milman is another product of Uppingham. On the opposite wing will be Cooke, a local boy, who played for Leicester a few times a couple of seasons ago. He had to give up the game on account of an injury to an arm. Otherwise, I think that the names are well known to Bedford supporters. |
| 17 | 5 | 8 | Let us examine the prospects of the Bedford Club in the light of what was revealed at the practice game last Saturday and let me give you one or two news items. First of all the trial impressions. Before the trial it was agreed that the prospects of the Club were bright and, with ordinary luck, another season of high performance was in store. After seeing the trial there is no reason to alter that opinion, which is not one of the usual pre-season optimistic sort based on hope rather than on knowledge. We all knew that the Club, with all last year’s men available, was in a happy position regarding playing strength, and Saturday’s practice revealed that there is an even greater wealth of talent than was expected. Let us take the two key positions at half-back to begin with. How many teams suffer through being poorly served at the link between pack and “threes”? A good many fifteens I’ll wager. Yet look at Bedford. Smyth, Staples, Lovelace, and Kenney (last year’s Leeds University team skipper) are all fitted to take their places at the base of the scrum or at fly-half, where Coleman is also available, not to mention players who normally figure at threequarter who are equally at home there. Likewise at three-quarter and forward the Club has no reason for anxiety with so many promising youngsters ready to challenge for places, and if Grey, at full-back, has the misfortune to get hurt or has to be absent from the team for any other reason, Coleman or Prigmore could well deputize for him. While on the subject of backs, I hear that it is possible Walshe may be joining the Fleet Air Arm, and that being so his appearances with the Bedford side may be limited. He will be missed very much if report is correct. And then there is Jimmy Power, a regular back of last season. I believe it is the Club’s intention to convert him into a forward. He played one game in the pack at the back end of last season and shaped very well. However, his defence as a back was very valuable last winter. Jimmy and his brother George were unable to take part in Saturday’s trial. George is recovering from tonsilitis. Other prominent players who missed the trial included Gilbert Cook, the first fifteen skipper, O. V. Bevan (vice-captain), V. J. Lyttle, D. L. K. Milman, G. T. Danger, A. E. Joy (who will not be able to play at Leicester), D. B. Coleman, C. G. Prigmore, G. L. B. August, and K. V. Percy, and yet the trial was eminently satisfactory. Not because the above players were not there, of course, but because there were so many keen, capable young thoroughbreds on view who kept the better-known players at good stretch. Assistant-Secretary Timmins refereed, and the two sides were in the charge of Rex Wilsher, as fit and wholehearted as ever, and Ralph Spark, the irrepressible, who gets as much fun out of the game as any other two players. |
| 17 | 6 | 10 | Wilsher had a strong array of tried hands under his command including Grey, Thevenard, Walshe, Rogers, Staples, Smyth, Kelly, McMaster, T. Hall, Irens, Black, Western—and there were some lively clashes and strong running. We saw some excellent pieces of work and some first-class tries. Thevenard, by virtue of his speed and indescribable wriggle of the body, having a generous helping of points. Rogers, Walshe, and Staples also crossed the line and both Irens and Grey added goal points with splendidly-judged kicks from wide out. All the old players showed that pace and skill were retained and that little needs remedying with the “bellows”. I don’t propose to go into detail beyond one or two observations on half-back play, which was interesting in view of the competition for places. Smyth, fully recovered from injury, and Staples quickly struck a happy understanding and should be the regular pair for the opening games of the season. Smyth has no equal in the Club for throwing such a long, accurate pass, and he moves quicker (with a long, raking stride) than is imagined. Staples was an outstanding figure at the trial and played with his head as well as his feet and hands. In giving this praise I am not detracting from the performances of Lovelace and Kenney. Lovelace was lively and enterprising, although he did not get so much of the ball as Smyth, and he will need a lot of holding down. He is full of good football. Kenney did not have an extended run at scrum-half, but when in that position he displayed likely form. His passing and generalship were good, but his transfers from the base of the scrum had not the length of the Smyth passes, which can be taken going forward; an important point. So now we must wait for Leicester—some of you Headingley—before we see the team have a real match test. It should be noted that the away fixture with Northampton has been transferred from Saturday 18th November to Thursday 5th October, as the Saturday date clashed with the Leicestershire and East Midlands match with the Australian tourists at Leicester. |
| 17 | 7 | 12 | It will be with great regret that Rugby supporters will hear that Trevor Thomas, the Welsh forward who gave such good service to the Bedford Club for several seasons, has had a foot amputated. Trevor, who lives at Woburn Sands, had one foot badly mutilated by machinery at Marston Valley Brickworks at Ridgemont while working on Thursday of last week. He is employed as a panfeeder. The machinery had to be stopped and reversed to release him. He was taken to the Bedford County Hospital, where the foot was amputated. |
| 18 | 2 | 6 | If Dorset had made an inauspicious start and finish, Berkshire were to do even worse. Few of the batsmen appeared to be comfortable, particularly against Bedham, whose deliveries came off the pitch pretty quickly and often popped up. With only one on the board, Berkshire lost their first wicket, and three of them were down for only 16 runs. Not until Crisfield and Cawston got together did runs come with any regularity. The latter got his 25 in eight hits, including a six. Magill rather foolishly threw his wicket away in tempting an almost impossible run. With the score at 74 for 7 at the tea interval, there were hopes of Berkshire at least getting somewhat near the Dorset score, but the remaining Berkshire wickets fell for two runs. |

| 19 | 1 | 6 | Cawston and Timewell opened the Berkshire innings, but the former was never really comfortable. It seemed a mistake to put a hitter in so early, especially as he had just completed bowling 19 consecutive overs. After having a snick at one or two balls which were well off the wicket, Cawston was caught behind the stumps. Stokes was getting going when Roberts came on with his spinners. Missing one of these, the Berkshire captain was well out of his crease when Grimsdell put down his wicket. A smart catch at mid-on disposed of Grisied, and Timewell went next, caught at silly mid-on in trying to hook a short ball to leg. Krabbe was, in fact, the only Berkshire batsman really to get going, and he contributed a valuable 42 before he was out to what was a sheer piece of bad luck. He put the ball round to the leg boundary, but in doing so stepped into his own wicket. The best assistance he received came from Girling, who bats left-handed but bowls right-handed. The innings closed with Berkshire being 43 runs in arrears. |

| 20 | 2 | 4 | Played at Cintra on Saturday, and, after an interesting game, ended in a win for Sutton's by 4 wickets, the winning hit being made in the last over of the match. Sutton's won the toss, and decided to put the Aspro in first. This policy was soon justified, as 5 wickets were down for 30, but then Keable and Blackman made a stand, and, with the tail wagging vigorously, they had made 171 when the last wicket fell. Keable played a valuable innings of 61. Anderson bowled 24 overs for 58 runs and 7 wickets—a very creditable performance. Against good bowling by Lund and Butler, Sutton's found runs difficult to obtain, but Cottrell came along with one of his best innings, and with Sayles (33), H. Bradfield (34 not out) and Croom (22) also playing good knocks, they were able to get the necessary runs for victory. This, however, was only possible by the sporting action of the Aspro side, who fielded during the last quarter of an hour in hard rain. Lund bowled excellently to take 4 wickets for 45, while Cottrell's innings of 63 was a splendid effort. |

| 21 | 2 | 7 | Stokes, the Berkshire captain, lost the toss for the third consecutive time, and Bucks went in to bat on what proved to be a batsmen's wicket. Play was delayed on Monday morning owing to the condition of the pitch following heavy rain, and sawdust had to be liberally applied even then to make it playable. Berkshire secured two easy wickets, but this brought Catchlife and Peters together to send the score along at a good pace. Peters, who was the top scorer of the match, was missed twice in one over from Bradley before he had reached double figures, first by Relf, who failed to reach a hard chance in the slips, and then by Caudle in the gully. This pair sent up the hundred in an hour and a quarter, and it was not until they had put on 92 runs that Relf made a brilliant catch in the slips to dispose of Catchlife. Walker joined Peters for this pair to take the score from 121 to 170, but thereafter wickets fell at fairly regular intervals, and the innings closed for 222. Some idea of the consistency of the Bucks batting may be gained from the fact that their first eight batsmen all reached double figures. |
| 21 | 4 | 9 | Left with 65 minutes for their second innings on Monday Berkshire lost four wickets for 58 runs, and an hour's play on Tuesday sufficed to finish the game. Except for one over by Edwards, Battcock and Rutter bowled throughout this innings unchanged, Rutter securing seven wickets for 55 runs. Some of the Berkshire players showed a tendency to play the ball with their pads instead of with the bat. Bayles was out in the second innings in this way, and Stokes was l.b.w. in both innings. In his second innings Stokes played the first ball he received with his pads, and there was a confident appeal against him. He was given not out on this occasion, but when he did almost exactly the same with the next ball the umpire gave a decision against him. Sutton and Wolton made the best stand, putting on 9 for the fifth wicket. Relf scored about the quickest 21 of the match, and it was a catch by a big hit from him which closed the match. |

| 21 | 39 | 80 | BERKSHIRE staged a splendid recovery in their Minor Counties match against Suffolk at Felixstowe on Wednesday. Batting first on an easy-paced wicket, they made a disastrous start, losing their first three wickets for 27 runs, but the later batsmen attacked the bowling and, thanks to their enterprise, the substantial total of 297 was reached. H. G. Hubble, with 12 fours as his best strokes, batted with admirable skill and initiative for his 88 runs, scored in 100 minutes. Daley, with 7 for 118, was Suffolk's most successful bowler. |
| 21 | 51 | 128 | Bowling—Relf 5 for 44, Tomlin 2 for 32, Cawston 3 for 43. |

| 23 | 1 | 5 | Some high scoring was witnessed at Cintra Ground, lent by Messrs. Sutton and Sons, when Berkshire entertained Suffolk for the first time at Reading in the Minor Counties Championship. This was the first county match to take place at Cintra for a number of years. Suffolk fielded a strong eleven, including seven players who had had experience of first-class county cricket, among them being Phil Mead, who played for England and Hampshire. ⏎ Winning the toss, Berkshire went in to bat on a very easy wicket. Runs came very rapidly at first, but wickets also fell fairly regularly. Stokes was clean bowled by a short-pitched ball from Belle, whilst Wolton was smartly stumped after going forward to a ball from the same bowler. It was not until Timewell and Crisfield came together that anything of a stand was made. They carried the score to 89 before Timewell put up a high catch in the outfield. Crisfield, who had been missed at four, followed soon afterwards, having contributed a very useful 40 to the score. Hubble started well, but played on to his wicket when he seemed to be getting set. The best batting display was given by Cawston, who in his 98 hit five 6's and eight 4's. He punished Daley very severely, hitting him for three sixes and a four off consecutive balls. Once Cawston had been disposed of, however, the Berkshire tail failed to wag, and the last three wickets fell for the addition of only five runs. Perkins had been the most successful Suffolk bowler. |
| 23 | 3 | 9 | Berkshire started their second innings disastrously, Stokes being stumped with only two on the board, and Timewell following nine runs later. Crisfield was the next victim, and the Suffolk bowlers seemed to be definitely on top. Until Hubble hit a four off Daley to make the score 26, all of the runs had come in singles. Hubble managed to stay in whilst two other wickets fell, but he was bowled eventually by a ball from Perkins, which broke back and took his leg stump. Taylor indulged in some hard hitting, but was given three lives early in his innings, and was eventually bowled by Daley. Relf was caught off the first ball he received. The Berkshire innings closed for 83 runs, and had lasted for only an hour and three-quarters. Perkins had the remarkable bowling figures of six for 15, and altogether had taken 12 wickets for 73 in the match. ⏎ With plenty of time in which to compile 102 runs for victory, Suffolk had obtained the runs by the tea interval for the loss of only two wickets. Suffolk, who are at the top of the Minor Counties championship table, have now played six matches, won four, and won one on the first innings, there being no result in one game. ⏎ Details:- BERKSHIRE-First Innings |

| 24 | 4 | 6 | With 91 runs to get to win, Bucks sent their opening batsmen in on an easy wicket, although the ball turned rather sharply at times. However, Franklin was out for 2, and Rachliffe and Baker put up a stubborn resistance until 29. Peters stayed long enough to make the Berkshire bowling look easy, and after lunch things began to happen. Bucks had to fight every inch of the way to get runs, and until Battcock arrived the game was in the balance. However, he settled the issue at shortly before 2.30 to give Bucks the win when he hit a four to square leg. |

| 25 | 4 | 36 | Played at Earley on Sunday, and won by Reading by 5 wickets. R.A.F. (Uxbridge) batted first, and Reading did very well to dismiss a strong batting side for 70 runs in 86 minutes, 7 wickets having fallen before lunch for 58 runs. Machray and Percival accounted for the opening pair in their first over, and J. W. Davis (5 overs, 4 maidens, 4 wickets, 6 runs) and C. J. Woodhead (8.3 overs, 1 maiden, 4 wickets, 22 runs) disposed of the rest of the side. Reading started very badly, losing 5 wickets for 39 in 55 minutes, but G. E. Neilan (43) and H. F. Fox (27) batted very well, and passed the R.A.F. total before being parted. Reading declared at 5.3 p.m. with the score 112 for 9, and R.A.F. (Uxbridge) then showed their batting quality, scoring 144 for 3 in 60 minutes. P/O. J. M. Jewell making 65, including 4 sixes and 5 fours. Reading replied with 100 for 7 in 68 minutes, having again started badly by losing 6 for 36. G. E. Neilan (14 not out) and J. W. Davis (34 not out) got the runs this time, and when stumps were drawn Reading were only 3 runs short of winning the two-innings match. |

| 26 | 7 | 39 | Buckinghamshire's fielding a season or two ago was frequently criticised. This season it's first-rate. |
| 26 | 8 | 47 | Capt. J. W. A. Stephenson, the Essex all-rounder, who formerly played for Beaconsfield and for Buckinghamshire, brought over a team whose constellation of stars—including G. O. Allen, D. R. Wilcox, R. W. V. Robins and S. R. Brown—was calculated to instil an inferiority complex into the home side. ⏎ But Beaconsfield, so far from being abashed by the formidable opposition, put up one of their best displays for years, C. G. Boddy defying the varied wiles of the visitors' attack until with only a few runs of his century. ⏎ A record crowd saw a neat game, and Windsor Hospital will benefit by £25 taken on the ground. |
| 26 | 10 | 53 | Brazil's ground is at Alltricks Farm, Little Kingshill, the home of Mr. Brazil, who takes a keen interest in the team's progress and watches all their games. |
| 27 | 7 | 8 | The home side again began their task badly, for Theobald, who opened the innings with Walker, was bowled by an inswinger from Lund with only 6 on the board. Walker and B. Rought-Rought, however, batted with great confidence, and Walker, in particular, made some beautiful strokes. The total had reached only 45 when he was bowled off his pads by the same bowler. G. Edrich was easily disposed of, but his brother E. H. Edrich, a fine forcing batsman, soon commenced hitting and the score mounted more quickly than it had at any time. He also hit Lund over the boundary, but in his attempt to force the game he was well caught in the slips by Lund off Rutter. The total mounted steadily until eight wickets were down for 153. Langdale, a left-hander, stood up manfully to the bowling, and the spectators followed the struggle with intense interest. Ballance, who had performed well in the first innings, was lured from his wicket by Edwards and easily stumped. With about ten minutes to go the last man came in—with 33 runs to get for victory. Despite all attempts to dislodge them they held out until time was called, when the score had reached 188 for nine wickets. |

| 32 | 12 | 41 | [RIGHT COLUMN] ⏎ Winning the toss the Brewery decided to put the home team in (incidentally, they are in the final of the Exeter League Shield) and soon found themselves in difficulties, five wickets going down for 12 runs. The whole side were all out in just over an hour for 54 runs. However, the visitors found that runs were not easy to get and lost seven wickets before their opponents' total was passed. When the last wicket fell (both teams played 12 men) the score had reached 94. ⏎ TEAMS ENTERTAINED ⏎ Following the match the teams were entertained at the Counters Wear Hotel when Mr. Dart, secretary to the Gas Co., and Mr. H. G. Read, on behalf of the Brewery, spoke of the way in which such fixtures between sports clubs promoted a better understanding between employees and hoped it would be possible for the fixtures between the Gas Co. and the Brewery to continue. In thanking their hosts for the very elaborate way in which the visitors had been looked after, Mr. Read thought they all ought to be very grateful to Mr. Loynes, hon. secretary of the Gas Co. Sports Club, for the very hard work he must have put in to make the visit such a success. ⏎ Four members of the bowling section of the Brewery took the opportunity of playing four members of the Gas Co. in the pleasant surroundings of the Exeter City Bowling Club, the game resulting in a win for the visitors by 21 shots to 9. ⏎ Exeter Gas Co.: H. | 34 | 25 | 29 | **FIVE WICKETS FOR FIVE RUNS!** ⏎ Twyning gave an excellent display on Saturday by beating the visiting side, Corse Lawn, by three wickets and 58 runs. Twyning batted first and knocked up 102 for seven, of which S. Healey contributed 51 not out. He was given good support by S. Schofield (13), A. Clements (19), and G. Hone (10). In spite of the considerable total, however, F. Cole (Corse Lawn) managed to take four wickets for only 20 runs, while L. Dudfield, W. Spry and O. Johnson captured one each for 15, 29, and 54 respectively. Corse Lawn had a chance of victory when O. Hartland scored 13, and F. Cole 16. But they failed to keep it up, and gave way under the pressure of S. Healey's excellent bowling. Corse Lawn was eventually dismissed for 44. S. Healey claimed five wickets for one run each, and C. Merrick two wickets for two runs. A. Schofield had two for seven, and A. Sowden one for 11. ⏎ **MATCHES CANCELLED** ⏎ The game arranged between Tewkesbury "A" and Evesham "A" was cancelled owing to Tewkesbury ground being unfit due to Friday's heavy rain. ⏎ The match between Longdon and Moore Cricket Club was cancelled owing to the latter being unable to raise a team. ⏎ **CAPTAIN'S TEAM BEATEN** ⏎ Bushley commenced its season on Saturday with a match between the captain's (E. E. Bisley) eleven and the sub-captain's (C. Walker) team, and after a pleasant and exciting game resulted in the latter team (although a man short) winning by one wicket and five runs. The captain's team went in first, and half the side was out for 33. S. Hunt was in good form, however, and contributed 22 (including a 6 and two 4's). The last man (J. W. Day) scored a valuable 14, and the team was dismissed for 85. C. Fowler was in good form with the ball, and captured the first four wickets for 15 runs; F. Mayo had three for 12; and P. Townsend three for eight. P. Hart and W. Barnes opened the account for the sub-captain's team, and thanks to the latter, 32 was on the board before a separation was effected. Thirty additional runs were on the score before the next wicket fell, and the fielding side looked like being beaten very badly. However, a slump took place and the following six wickets added 26 only. But this was sufficient to gain victory, Barnes contributed 40 not out, including five 4's; and J. F. Fowler scored 17. E. E. Bisley, for the captain's eleven, captured four wickets for 23, and J. W. Day, three for seven. ⏎ **OVERBURY BEATEN AT HOME** ⏎ Overbury and Buckland and Laverton opened their season on Saturday when they met at Overbury, the visitors winning by 12 runs. Batting first, Buckland knocked up 73, G. Hacklett hitting a useful 38. Overbury could only muster 61 in reply despite a stand of 22 by W. Powell. For Overbury, A. Perks captured four for nine and E. Stanley four for 32, the Buckland bowlers being R. Bryant (4 for 21), H. Green (4 for 26), and J. Collis (2 for 12). ⏎ Scores:— ⏎ Buckland and Laverton: E. Townsend b Perks 2, G. Hacklett c Powell b Stanley 38, J. Collis b Powell 14, H. Green c Digby b Powell 4, E. Green b Perks 5, J. Grimmett b Stanley 3, R. Grimmett b Perks 1, C. Bryant b Stanley 0, T. Bryant not out 0, D. Bryant b Stanley 1, R. Bryant b Perks 3, extras 2, total 73. ⏎ Overbury: G. Greaves b R. Bryant 16, A. Perks b Collis 2, H. Key c E. Green b H. Green 0, W. Willmore c E. Townsend b H. Green 0, G. Wise c R. Grimmett b R. Bryant 0, W. Powell lbw b R. Bryant 22, P. Wesson c H. Green b R. Bryant 1, E. Stanley c Hacklett b H. Green 7, J. Smith lbw b H. Green 6, S. Digby not out 3, R. Windfield c H. Green b Collis 3, extras 1, total 61. ⏎ **RIPPLE WIN BY NINE RUNS.** |
| 34 | 26 | 47 | **HASFIELD AND TIRLEY LOSE FIRST GAME** ⏎ Hasfield and Tirley opened their season on Saturday by being defeated at Norton by one wicket and 19 runs. ⏎ The visitors, who took first knock, began moderately well, T. Dudfield and D. Barnes scoring 19 between them. The scoring rather sagged after this, and five wickets were down for only 27 runs. L. Bond contributed another ten, and the remaining batsmen being out, the visitors closed their innings with a total of 44 only. ⏎ Norton's victory was in no small measure due to the 23 runs contributed by A. Bradshaw, because no other player with the exception of A. Maycott (12 not out) reached double figures. The issue was put beyond all doubt with three wickets in hand, and the Norton score closed at 63 for nine. ⏎ For Norton, F. Wheeler had the splendid analysis of six wickets for 10, L. Gardiner capturing the remaining four for 14 runs. ⏎ No fewer than seven bowlers were tried by the visitors, of whom G. Didcote was most successful with four wickets for nine; J. W. Bartlett had three for 12, and W. Steward and T. Dudfield one each for eight and nine respectively. ⏎ **FORTHAMPTON C.C.** ⏎ As it was found impossible, owing to lack of players, to carry on Forthampton Cricket Club this season, the fixtures have been cancelled. At a meeting of members on Wednesday evening, an inventory of the spoilt view to their being disposed of, but it was stated the club was only in abeyance, and it was hoped that it would be re-started in the future. Meanwhile, it was decided that the assets of the club should be invested in the hands of one or more trustees, so that, if revived, it would have a sound financial basis. |

| 38 | 3 | 7 | To-morrow the XI will be at Worthing Sussex are due for a come back. Will this be the occasion? For a good many years past Sussex has more often than not been well on top in their games with Gloucestershire. |
| 38 | 6 | 10 | The original list of members and their addresses are set out in full. They include many familiar names—Wilterforce, Tribe, and Frank Wills, who later became Sir Frank, being among them No Bush's appear J. A. and R. E. were at that time playing for Bedminster and joined Clifton when the first club in Bristol broke up. |
| 38 | 8 | 12 | The report states that the touchdowns were secured for Bedminster by S W L. Llewellyn, G. H. Pepler and W H. Brown. The last named was for many years chairman of the Bristol Cricket Association and father of W S. A. Brown, a fine player for Gloucestershire under Dr. W G. Grace and Gilbert Jessop. |
| 38 | 11 | 18 | The paragraph stated:—"If any of our readers have ought against the Warwickshire Cricket Club Committee, and would wreak a deadly revenge, let him murmur in their ears the one word 'Cranston.' The result is instantaneous paralysis. Three years ago Mr James Cranston dropped the residential qualification under which he had for several years played for Gloucestershire. He had been distinctly the best left-handed batsman of the day. He had a birth qualification for Warwickshire" ⏎ "The committee played him three times, twice against Surrey and once against the Australians, and then dropped him, almost contemptuously. This year he has again a qualification for Gloucestershire, and has been eagerly welcomed to the team. He has come off in every match, and his batting, so far, has been quite one of the features of the season." |
| 38 | 14 | 28 | Mr Kitcat had another memorable experience with Dr. W. G. Grace. He was in the team during that wonderful May, 1895, when the "Old Man" made cricket history by scoring 1,000 runs before the month was over. At Gravesend, when Dr Grace was in the field the whole of the three days, his best partner in making 257 runs was S. A. P. Kitcat, who played a fine innings of 52, when other members of the team, except Jack Painter, found runs hard to get. |
| 39 | 1 | 0 | STROUD NEWS: FRIDAY 7,14 JULY 1939.-2. |
| 39 | 10 | 31 | STANCOMBE PARK v. SHELL MEX ⏎ (Bristol) ⏎ In an interesting game at Stancombe Park the home side defeated Shell Mex by 12 runs. Stancombe batted first, but only D. Clark (28), J. S. Hughes (25) and W. Beale (11) eached double figures, the remaining batsmen falling on a difficult wicket. Shell Mex started disastrously. Beale in his opening over hitting the stumps on three occasions. The visitors' middle batsmen batted stubbornly, but their last wicket fell at 66 despite a good knock by Brinton. Scores:— ⏎ STANCOMBE PARK |

| 42 | 1 | 0 | WESTERN DAILY PRESS: MONDAY 21 AUGUST 1939-1. |
| 42 | 5 | 5 | Bedminster took first knock and were indebted chiefly to L. Champeny (53), and R. J. Simons (42 not out). The Y.M.C.A. had an hour and three-quarters in which to get the runs, and collected the first 100 in just under the hour. H. Rowlands was in fine form with 52 not out, while C. J. Axe (45), D. C. Johnson (33), and V. R. Davies (25), batted exceptionally well. |
| 42 | 6 | 6 | A fine century by Rupert C. Robinson, who hit up 101, not out, enabled Flax Bourton to declare at 210 runs for five wickets, and was largely instrumental in his side's overwhelming victory against Stoke Bishop. |
| 42 | 10 | 10 | United Banks lost two wickets for 14 runs, but with M. Arnold and Tanner together Stinchcombe's total was passed. Tanner had a wonderful match, following up his splendid bowling with a great half-century. |
| 42 | 11 | 12 | Bridgwater started steadily, P. Thorn and D. Norton scoring 31 and 25 runs respectively, and then came a hurricane innings by A. M. Pearce of 67 runs in 28 minutes, including 11 fours and a six. While Pearce and K Cotham (31) were together 83 runs were added. |
| 42 | 12 | 13 | Schoolmasters scored a fine victory over Taunton, on the latter's ground, by six wickets. Taunton went in first and made a strong start, S. Wiggin making 31 and H. Fear, 34. Taunton declared at 129 runs for nine wickets. |
| 42 | 14 | 16 | It is not often that a tie occurs, but such was the result at Coombe Dingie, in the match between Bohemians and Frys. |
| 42 | 15 | 17 | Frys batted first, and thanks to A. Woolacott (64, not out), and L. H. Carter (36), they totalled 130. Then the Bohemians made a poor start, losing two wickets for a few runs, but A. Bull (21), C. R. Lucas (38), and H. J. Alpass (34), pulled things round. However, the bowlers got on top again, and with the last pair together it was anybody's game. Amid great excitement R. F. P. Holloway levelled the scores, but in attempting the winning run, the last man was run out, and the game ended at 130 runs apiece. P. W. Setchell, and R. D. French took four wickets for 28 runs, and three for 21 respectively, for Bohemians. |
| 42 | 19 | 21 | Making his first appearance this season, for Westbury, E. H. Staddon scored 20, not out, and took four wickets for 22, and so helped materially in his side's easy victory over Clifton, at Henbury. Westbury took first knock, and totalled 146. |
| 42 | 21 | 23 | The Optimists beat Claverham by 33 runs at Blaise Castle. C. F. Bolt scored 43 for the Optimists, out of a total of 115, and in reply Claverham could only total 62 runs. |
| 42 | 22 | 24 | In a benefit match for George Hunt, the Weston-s.-Mare groundsman and player, Weston beat Chard by 88 runs. Consistent batting by Weston enabled them to declare at 191 runs for five wickets, G. Hunt scoring 37, and W. W. Birtill 58. L. C. Hawkins and Hunt bowled so consistently, getting six and four wickets respectively, that Chard were dismissed for 103 of which H. F. Golley made 32 |

| 46 | 2 | 6 | North Met. Bricket Wood and (St. Albans) 87 .. Munden 83 ⏎ J. Illingworth was put on to bowl too late. He went on with the score at 80; only one more run was added. Illingworth took the last three wickets with the first three balls of his second over. T. Palmer (10), W. Clark (13), and H. Goldup (21) gave Bricket Wood and Munden a good start against a total of 87, but the tail failed, and North Met. just got home by four runs. |
| 46 | 7 | 19 | After Argile (120), Hickson (31), and Scott (34 not out) had taken full toll of the Taylors' bowling, Aldenham left their opponents two hours in which to get the runs. With victory an impossibility, the Taylor's did the next best thing and avoided defeat. |
| 46 | 9 | 25 | Remarkable bowling by Plested skittled out Berkhamsted II at Berkhamsted. Plested bowled Todd and after Parry had dealt with Haslam and Weatherill, who, with Todd, were the only players to score, Plested clean bowled the remaining seven batsmen without addition to the score. Plested bowled 34 balls and took eight wickets for the cost of one run! |
| 46 | 12 | 33 | Abbots Langley II Rickmansworth II (10 wkts.) 148 .. (5 wkts. dec.) 206 ⏎ Rickmansworth had the best of the game at Abbots Langley. Batting first, they scored 206 for five declared, Argile, hitting hard, scored 105 (1° 4's). |
| 46 | 13 | 36 | L.P.T.B. Garage Amersham-Hill 81 .. Hemel Hempstead 98 ⏎ Played at Amersham on Thursday, the Passenger Transport Board's side won by 17 runs. For Amersham Martin bowled well, taking six for 27, including the "hat trick," and for the Garage Shearn took seven for 51. ⏎ BUSHEY II'S GOOD RECORD ⏎ Harpenden II 123 .. Bushey II 134 |
| 46 | 15 | 48 | Balham Park Nash Mills 50 & 32 .. 77 & (no wkt.)6 |
| 46 | 16 | 49 | Balham won this first match which although ending in a comfortable ten wicket win had Turner to thank, making a hurricane 51 (four o's and one 4) |

| 48 | 1 | 0 | HERTS ADVERTISER: FRIDAY 4 AUGUST 1939-2. |
| 48 | 3 | 3 | The Rev. E. T. Killick, the England Middlesex and Cambridge University player, batted splendidly for Letchworth at Clarence Park, on Saturday. He had made 72 not out when Letchworth, who batted first, declared with 153 for 4. A S. Hacker (28) and G. F. Beaumont (27), St. Albans' opening pair, put on 57 before they were parted, but after that wickets fell quickly and at the close of the game, which was left drawn, St. Albans had made 92 for 5. |
| 48 | 4 | 4 | Scores:- ⏎ Letchworth. ⏎ T. T. Dias, c Minshull, b S. Rabone .. 7 |
| 48 | 6 | 10 | G. Kay, c sub., b Minshull ... 24 |

| 48 | 12 | 22 | E. H. Rabone, c Feider, b Smith .. 13 |
| 48 | 16 | 29 | St. Albans beat the Old Albanians by three wickets at Clarence Park, on Sunday. With J. T. Marriott, who took 6 wickets for 22 runs in eight overs, bowling well, St. Albans dismissed the Old Boys for 85, a collapse ensuing after the sixth wicket fell at 74. When St. Albans had seven wickets down for 50, a close finish seemed likely, but W. Lismore (26 not out) and G. Weatherhead (12) knocked off the runs without further loss. |
| 48 | 17 | 30 | Scores:- ⏎ Old Albanians. |
| 48 | 18 | 33 | F. F. George, b Minshull .. 3 |

| 48 | 39 | 61 | Herts will be without P. G. T. Kingsley, the skipper, who is in camp with the Territorials, for their matches with Norfolk, at Norwich, on Monday and Tuesday, and with Bedfordshire, at Bedford, on Friday and Saturday. Arthur Grimsdell will captain the team, which will be chosen from: A. Grimsdell, G. E. Fordham, F. Appleyard, G. W. Ford, F. K. Butler, M. A. Wiggs, G. V. Ledger, G. J. Adams, M. H. Kingsley, A. E. Tee, E. L. Lane and Roberts (E.). |
| 48 | 41 | 63 | L. O. King, of Radlett, performed the "hat trick" for the Herts County Club on Friday, when they beat St. Albans by 184 runs. Taking seven wickets for 33, he was mainly responsible for St. Albans' cheap dismissal. King also batted well, his 67 being the top score of the game. The Country Club lost two wickets for 14, but J. A. R. Oliver (56) and King, whose partnership realised 104, won the game without further loss. |

| 48 | 62 | 96 | The County Club's match at Halleybury ended in a draw. The College batted first and the game opened sensationally, for Oliver got Chanter bowled off the first ball and had Souter caught at the wicket after three runs had been scored, but the third-wicket partnership realised 118, and Halleybury declared with 194 for 6. The County Club went for the runs, but a couple of stoppages owing to rain thwarted them, for at the close, with 6 wickets in hand, they still required 25 for victory. Scores:- |

| 48 | 77 | 120 | St. Albans' second team lost their return match with Dickinson's, at Aspley, on Saturday, by one wicket after an exciting finish. I. H. Crocker (16) and D. B. Turner (14) put on 32 for St. Albans' first wicket, but the side collapsed after that and the innings closed for 65. Dickinson's had eight wickets down for 46, but D. Clarke (28 not out) played soundly. When the last man went in, four runs were still needed and, amid excitement, were secured. Scores:- |

Boon (37) put on 89 in a second-wicket partnership for Cell Barnes, while E. G. Cotton (28) and J. Morley (10) stopped the rot when St. Albans had lost four wickets for 15. Scores:- |

| 48 | 111 | 180 | Northmet (St. Albans) beat Frogmore by eleven runs at Park Street, on Sunday. They made 86, of which R. Levin (26 not out) and J. Smith (4) contributed 50 in a last-wicket stand. O. Butler (21), B. Smith (15), D. P. Hollingsworth (10) made most of the runs previously. Wright (4-18) and J. England (4-34) did best with the ball for Frogmore, while L. Lee (25) batted well, but the rest of the side did badly until W. Lee (18) and A. Rimmonds (5 not out) put on 27 for the last wicket, which lifted the total to 75. F. Runchiman (5-17), L. For their successful reply to Leighton Buzzard's score, they were chiefly indebted to H. H. Grange, who batted No. 2, and scored 51 runs without being defeated. | 53 | 16 | 94 | Thanks to a display of consistent batting, in which Simons, Briers, Glenister, and Carlisle particularly shone, Abbots Langley were able to declare at 160 for eight wickets in their match with the Old Albanians at St. Albans. The home team, however, was never in danger of defeat, Griffiths and George, the opening pair, batting confidently, especially the former, whose 61 not out was an enterprising effort. Fenton, of the Old Albanians, was the only outstanding bowler, capturing five wickets for 36 runs in 12 overs. Score: |

During the day 314 runs were scored for the loss of 35 wickets. Rickmansworth batted first, and thanks to a good 37 by Barnes they made 90. ⏎ Hockey, the old Essex player, taking five for 18. ⏎ When the Old Sandronians batted they found runs harder to get, and only Hockey was able to make any scoring strokes against the Rickmansworth attack. Hinton was nearly unplayable, and had the fine analysis of five wickets for three runs. Score: |

| 54 | 1 | 0 | Berkhamsted Wilt Before ⏎ Leg-Break Barnes |
| 54 | 3 | 3 | BENNETT, a 15-years-old schoolboy, going in first for Rickmansworth, helped Openshaw to put on 59 for the first wicket. These runs later proved invaluable, ⏎ as wickets began to tumble regularly, especially against Stelling (six for 33). |
| 54 | 4 | 6 | When Berkhamsted faced the home attack they fared even worse, and found the leg breaks of Barnes more than they could master. Barnes had six for 25, and Berkhamsted were all out for 66--73 short of Rickmansworth's total. Score: |

| 54 | 35 | 72 | Radlett were given first use of a tricky wicket and K. H. Chapman had the new ball. He took a wicket with his first ball, and in his first five overs took five wickets without conceding a run. In all he had seven for eight. Set to score 46 runs, Stanmore were soon in difficulties, and lost five wickets before they won the match. T. Down, of Radlett, too, found the conditions favourable to his art, and took six wickets for 25 runs. Score: |

| 55 | 1 | 0 | A. W. BRIERS—R. CROWNSHAW AXIS AGAIN! |
| 55 | 2 | 2 | ABBOTS LANGLEY'S victory at Welwyn by 58 runs on Sunday was chiefly due to the remarkable bowling of A. W. Briers, who, in the course of seven overs, captured six wickets for nine runs. He was ably supported by R. Crownshaw, who in eight overs secured four wickets for 21 runs. ⏎ Abbots Langley batted first, and were soon in difficulties with O'Brien, who bowled magnificently in capturing six wickets for 19 runs. A. W. Briers and C. Sharp saved the situation with scores of 25 and 27 respectively, but even so, a total of 90 appeared insufficient. However, the home batsmen were unable to cope with the Briers—Crownshaw combination, and were dismissed in 54 minutes for a meagre 32. Score:— |
| 55 | 3 | 6 | The County Club won by 181 runs at St. Albans. Score:— |

| 56 | 6 | 38 | No cricket was possible at Rickmansworth over the Bank Holiday until 4 o'clock on Monday. The match had been scratched at lunch time, but the weather improved, and the captains decided to make a start. The Rickmansworth team was made up at the last minute (as several people had left the ground at lunch time), and they could only find nine batsmen. Old Spicers batted first and made 91, the last wicket putting on 27. Murray hit 2 for 37 and Edmunds bowled well, taking seven for 21. When Rickmansworth batted Marriott played well for 29, and with Chicken had the best stand of the day, but Rickmansworth were all out for 74, losing by 17 runs. Score: |

| 59 | 2 | 2 | A dogged last-wicket stand by C. J. S. Fltoot and I. W. Houghton saved the Old Berkhamstedians from defeat in their match with the Herts County Club at Bucklands on Friday. W. Brian Smith (48) and R. L. Eade (73) batted well for the County Club. Wilson, who was averaging not much more than three since, and A. M. Olsson (57) gave the Old Berkhamstedians a good start, but after they had been separated wickets fell quickly, the ninth falling when the Old Boys were 42 in arrears. Fltoot and Houghton, however, defended doggedly to save the game. Scores:— |

| 59 | 62 | 124 | The Old Albanians suffered a sensational one-run defeat on Saturday, when they opposed Shenley Hospital at Porter's Park. Shenley, thanks largely to a good knock of 42 by A. Clarke, totalled 98. M. A. Wiggs (56), A. G. Owen (18) and K. A. Griffiths (16) gave the Old Boys a capital start; indeed they needed only nineteen for victory when the fourth wicket fell, but Brown (4—38) and Woodcock (2—21) brought about a surprising collapse. Eight wickets were down for 99. The ninth lifted the total to 97 and two runs were still required when W. Fenton, the last man, went in, but he was caught by Greenhaugh of Brown without addition to the total and Shenley succeeded, in a tense finish, by one run. Scores:— |

Rigby did not bat. ⏎ Bowling: Meyer 2-10, Nicholls 1-14. ⏎ RADLETT'S LAST PAIR FOIL HARPENDEN. ⏎ The return match between Radlett and Harpenden, at Cobden Hill, Radlett, on Saturday, was left drawn with Radlett failing hard to avert defeat, being 31 behind with one wicket to fall when time came. ⏎ Harpenden made 119 against good-length bowling by T. Down (3-53) and L. G. King (4-54). They were chiefly indebted to R. A. Day, who made 59 by patient batting, and D. C. L. Mole, who contributed 33. ⏎ Radlett had five wickets down for 21 and seven men out for 38, while the total had reached only 65 when the last pair—F. G. Taylor (11 not out) and F. Green (12 not out) came together. They defied all attempts to dislodge them and added 23. J. W. Bewell, who made 18, showed great promise for Radlett. R. A. Day, who took 5 wickets for 22 in the course of fourteen overs, of which six were maiden, did best with the ball for Harpenden, although W. H. Marsh (4-51) again bowled very steadily. Scores: |

| 61 | 16 | 30 | WEDNESDAY'S WIN ON SUNDAY! |
| 61 | 17 | 31 | Sandridge again disappointed their supporters on Sunday by losing their home game with Hadlett Wednesday by 44 runs. |
| 61 | 18 | 32 | Dismissed in ten overs for 32, no member of the Sandridge team reached double figures, Cautsin (9) and Roan (8) being top scorers. Hadlett scored 26 of the necessary runs before the first wicket fell, Sampson going on first change then secured 3 wickets for 6 runs, finishing up with 4 for 12. W. Lamb subsequently hit out, and he and Hemslcy added 21 for the last wicket. The fact that two Sandridge players one their principal run-getter and the other who was holding up his end nicely were run out had disastrous effect on the side, but the bowling of Brennan was exceptionally good, as his figures, 5—13, show. Scores:- |

| 61 | 99 | 160 | Elstree lost unexpectedly to the Old Johnians on Saturday. E. Evans (6—32) was in great form with the ball and the Old Boys, who batted first, had a disastrous time. The first wicket fell at 11 and seven men were out for 14. P. Lumley (10) helped E. Keeton (36) to stop the rot, but the eighth wicket fell at 24. J. Stieb (15) and Keeton put on the total of 48 for 9, and J. Leach, who made one not out, kept his end up while Keeton lifted the total to 74 before being bowled by J. Milner. |
| 61 | 100 | 161 | W. H. Ridout (22) and J. Milner (10) put on 30 in an opening partnership for Elstree, but things went badly after that and five men were out for 36. A. Willshire (15) lifted the score to 54 for 6, but D. Kerans (6—37) and C. Leigh (4—17) got on top again and the innings closed for 62. Scores:- |

| 63 | 32 | 66 | SANDRIDGE BEAT REFEREES. ⏎ Two veterans figured prominently in a low-scoring game at Sandridge, where the Mid Herts Referees lost by 24 runs on Saturday. ⏎ Batting first the Referees were dismissed for 35, A. C. Sampson obtaining 4 for 8, Winch 2 for 6, Atkins 1 for 0 and Caustin 1 for 9. This score should have been easy for Sandridge, but they found Eames bowling his breaks with his old-time accuracy and seven wickets were down for 21, Caustin (17 not out) and Sampson, however, added 19 for the eighth wicket and Caustin afterwards hit out, obtaining 16 in one over, and the innings closed for 59. Clay (17) played a sound defensive innings for the Referees. Scores:— ⏎ Mid Herts Referees. ⏎ W. Clay, b Caustin .. 17 ⏎ A. Thompson, b Winch .. 4 ⏎ E. Draper, b Winch .. 0 ⏎ K. | 63 | 44 | 101 | King's Langley beat Berkhamsted by 25 runs at King's Langley on Saturday, which was a very creditable performance after an indifferent start. Eight wickets were down for 39, but then J. F. Scott (33) joined W. J. Townley, who carried his bat for 49, and the total reached 120. So far this season, Townley has made 584 runs, including two centuries for King's Langley, B. W. Atkins (4—20), J. F. Scott (5—55) and Edmonds (2—29) dismissed Berkhamsted for 87. Scores:— |

| 64 | 1 | 0 | WEST HERTS AND WATFORD OBSERVER: FRIDAY 18 AUGUST 1939-3 ⏎ LORD'S STAFF PLAYER HITS ⏎ 101 AFTER SHOCKS |
| 64 | 2 | 4 | IN half-an-hour Lord's Ground Staff lost three wickets for 35 runs; all fell to K. H. Chapman, who hit the stumps with his first ball. Haycraft offered sterner ⏎ resistance, but two wickets fell before lunch, when the score was 110 for five wickets. Haycraft went on to make his century and, supported by the later batsmen, they batted briskly and showed great respect for the bowling of Windham Griffiths, who in 15 overs conceded only 17 runs and bowled out two of the more successful batsmen. ⏎ Stanmore were set to score 255 runs in three hours, normally an easy task but here the bowling proved to be too accurate to allow quick scoring with safety, and the game finished in a draw. Score: ⏎ LORD'S GROUND STAFF ⏎ R. Purner, b Chapman .. 6 ⏎ J. Attwell, b Griffiths .. 29 ⏎ J. Eaglestone, b Chapman .. 4 |

| 64 | 17 | 35 | ANOTHER DON YATES CENTURY ⏎ Hill End Hospital 23 .. (10 wkts., dec.) 156 ⏎ Don Yates was at it again on Saturday with a fine innings of 108 not out. He and D. Edis (44) scored 125 runs without being separated. When Hill End Hospital went in to bat, they could do nothing against the spin bowling of H. Beach (four for 5) and J. Prescott (four for 4), for they were all out for 25. Score: ⏎ Odhams.—Don Yates, not out, 108. D. Edis, not out, 44. Extras, 4. Total (no wkts.), 156. J. Prescott, H. Beach, T. James, A. Halsey, A. | 67 | 20 | 44 | STARRED SELECTIONS ⏎ DON YATES, who is the terror of every local bowler, hit his 44th century on Saturday. Since 1928, when he started his brilliant career with Watford Clarence, Yates has been a prolific scorer. With Oxhey Grange, Watflows, Oddhams and North Watford Conservatives, he has hit 1,000 runs (or more) each year since 1929. He has already reached this number this season. One summer on behalf of Watflows his tally reached 2,000. ⏎ His average to date for this wet summer is 67. ⏎ What a great pity it was that Yates's talents were not recognised, and some effort made to give him an opportunity to develop in a higher class of cricket. It is obvious that Yates has exceptional ability, and would have made his mark in higher company had he been brought along on the right lines. ⏎ It seems a crying shame that such talent is wasted—it wouldn't happen in Australia. ⏎ The excellent batting of W. Townley, of Kings Langley, has been attracting a lot of attention. He is well worth a trial for the county. ⏎ Less able players than he have often appeared. ⏎ An indication of this young man's ability was furnished on Saturday when he carried his bat for 49 against Berk- hamsted—not a great score, it is true, but his innings won the match for his side. A type of opening bat that is rare in club cricket, Townley is dependable, thorough and workmanlike. ⏎ Best individual performances were: ⏎ BATTING ⏎ D. YATES (Oldhams) v. Hill End Hospital—103 not out. ⏎ P. HAYCROFT (Lord's Ground Staff) v. Stanmore—101. ⏎ BOWLING ⏎ D. YATES (Oldhams) v. Hill End Hospital—seven for 20. ⏎ F. COOPER (Sun H) v. Bricket Wood and Munden—four for 0. ⏎ INDEPENDENTS PUZZLED BY WALKER ⏎ Nash Mills 84 Independents 89 ⏎ A game worth playing, Independents batted doggedly for their first innings score, being always puzzled by the clever bowling of S. | 68 | 23 | 39 | The visit of Chipperfield to Abbots Langley on Saturday provided the most thrilling game seen on the Manor Ground this season, for with only two wickets to fall the home side required 17 runs to win, and these were obtained without further loss, although at the finish only 8 runs separated the two scores. ⏎ For Chipperfield only R. Smith and Joiner batted with confidence, the former scoring an attractive 67 in a total of 125. R. Crownshaw again bowled well in taking five wickets for 35, and Glenister, too, had the fine analysis of four wickets for 22 runs. Abbots Langley seemed well set for victory after scoring 84 for three wickets, Jones playing a really fine innings of 53, but eight wickets were down for 109, and until Arnott and P. Briers became associated, the home record of freedom from defeat this season seemed lost. Goodman bowled exceptionally well for Chipperfield, taking five wickets for 35 in 17 overs. Score: |

| 69 | 22 | 63 | Rickmansworth entertained the London Aus- tralians (who last year called themselves Aus- tralia House) on Sunday, and lost by 44 runs. The home side batted first and scored only 127 on a good hard wicket. Shepherd and Odam's were the best performers in the Rick- mansworth side. At tea, London Australians had lost five wickets for 63 and the game appeared to be very even, but Hall and Scrivener dug themselves in and scored in singles until well set, took the score to 140 before Scrivener was bowled for 31. Score: |
| 69 | 30 | 92 | Useful Team to Test Herts! ⏎ Denis Compton, the England and Middlesex cricketer, is bringing an International team to meet a Herts XI, at the Old Albanians ground, St. Albans, on Sunday, September 3, for Ted Roberts' benefit fund. Compton will captain a side which will be selected from the following: J. M. Sims, W. J. Edrich and F. Price (England and Middlesex), J. Hardstaff (England and Notts), W. E. WEMBLEY (3 wkts.) 213 ⏎ WEMBLEY turned the tables on Rickmansworth in the return match at Wembley on Saturday. Shepherd, Cornwell, and Odam's all batted well. Wem- bley opened with a stand of 94 in an hour before Molony was bowled for 48; he hit hard but gave several difficult chances. | 70 | 4 | 3 | THE BATTING in this match was always entertaining. Lambkin profited by his early caution while some of his partners, who hit out from the word "go," paid the penalty of exuberance. The model of batsmanship came from W. T. Palmer. His runs were scored all by clean, powerful shots to all parts of the field. Stanmore's main support was F. Richards, who again helped to retrieve fortunes after a bad start. Marsland was the only bowler to enjoy continued success for the wicket was always on the side of the batsmen. Score:- |
| 70 | 5 | 4 | STARRED SELECTIONS ⏎ IN the ideal cricket weather that only the start of the football season can bring, few matches were brought to a definite result. Abbots Langley preserved their unbeaten record by the skin of their teeth, beating Chipperfield by eight runs. Hemel Hempstead won very comfortably, but West Herts, Bushey, Chorleywood, Stanmore, Leavesden and Kings Langley were among the teams that drew their fixtures. ⏎ Of the five centuries that were scored, Don Yates obtained two—hitting 108 not out on Saturday, and 100 not out on Sunday. Yet another Yates achievement. ⏎ The best individual performances were as follows: ⏎ BATTING ⏎ G. FLOYD (West Watford Conserva-tives) v. Northwood British Legion 119. ⏎ D. YATES (Odhams) v. Rembrandt —108*. ⏎ M. B. FAITHFULL (Bushey II) v. Chorleywood II—105. ⏎ G. ELOICE (Sun Engraving) v. Aylesford Paper Mills—104. ⏎ D. YATES (North Watford Conserva-tives) v. Ogee Sports—100*. ⏎ * Denotes not out. ⏎ BOWLING ⏎ R. BRANDON (Hemel Hempstead) v. Leighton Buzzard—7 for 28. ⏎ H. FORD (St. John's) v. Leavesden-road Baptists—6 for 5. ⏎ P. | 70 | 40 | 103 | West Herts, one man short, did nothing in this match at Raynes Park to enhance their reputation. Missed catches and poor fielding helped to swell their opponents' score to 100 for nine, when the closure was applied. ⏎ Replying, West Herts were given a good send off by Lane and Dodman, who put on 45, but apart from Kendrick's modest 16, the rest batted with caution. | 73 | 9 | 37 | Opposing Bedford, on the latter’s ground, Hitchin had an early shock when J. D. Waters was ibw to Kenney for a duck with only one run on the score-board. W. V. Stanley then helped King to take the score to 132 before being caught, and when King left four runs later he had made 102 out of the 138. Subsequently Sid Brown made 35 (which included six boundaries) and Barker hit five four’s in a useful contribution of 26, so that Hitchin finally totalled 222. |
| 73 | 15 | 53 | Dearden also bowled with success at the start of the Hertford innings, and he and Scott had dismissed eight men for only 80 when Clibbon and Ellis made a stand which carried the home side to a one-wicket win. |
| 73 | 18 | 56 | The United’s victory was an exciting one, being principally due to a last-wicket stand by Armitage and B. Bridges, the latter making an invaluable 13 not out. A Mead, of the Hitchin team, was the most successful bowler, with 6 for 37. |
| 73 | 19 | 58 | In their last home game, Kimpton suffered a heavy defeat against Luton Boys’ Club, and from a results point of view they have had an unusually poor season. Bowling unchanged for the visitors, Matthews and Jones ran through the Kimpton team for the meagre score of 45, and only Gerald Cain, who hit a six, got double figures. Slack fielding was partly responsible for the Luton team making 80 for five wickets, although two of their players were run out in attempting to steal short runs. |
| 73 | 20 | 61 | E. Harper captured seven wickets for 21 runs in helping Offley to beat Luton Hoo by 95 to 62. For the losers, Branner also took seven wickets, at a cost of 39 runs. |
| 73 | 22 | 66 | Ransom’s were out for 55, Massey taking 5 for 28 and Darlow 5 for 12, and the Icknield lads were then disposed of for a mere 25, J. Sworder (who, incidentally, made top score of 17 for his side) taking 6 for 6, and J. Chappelle 2 for 10. Seven of the eleven players got ducks! |

| 76 | 22 | 103 | Next in the averages is R. H. Valentine, and here again we had splashes of brilliance interspersed with failures. He is one of those batsmen feared by the opposite side. When once he gets going all bowlers seem powerless, but his great failure is a desire to start scoring a little too quickly, and for that reason he has lost his wicket many a time in the first few overs. |
| 76 | 24 | 105 | Chalk is a great personality in the field, always alert and beloved by the whole team. Like our other bat-men, he had long spells of low scoring, but his three centuries were an exhibition of sparkling cricket. When out to make runs, Chalk, Valentine and Ames are a treat to watch. No other county possesses such a trio. ⏎ When young Peter Foster came into the team a good many judges of the game said he was not quite up to first-class form and in the first three matches he lived up to this reputation. In the middle of the season, however, he greatly improved and his 107 against Leicester was a delightful innings. You could not at present call his defence very sound, but he possesses some charming strokes. ⏎ Todd has maintained his position as the most valuable all-round man in the eleven. If anything he has been over-bowled and it must be remembered that he is the first to attack against the best batsmen. His batting has naturally suffered, but at times he has heroically stopped a collapse. And three centuries. A fine record. ⏎ The rest of our batsmen have been disappointing. Wright and Harding have not come up to expectations in that part of the game. ⏎ Coming to the bowling, we find Wright far ahead of the others. At times he simply slaughtered batsmen. He has increased his pace this year and the quickness with which his break comes in from the leg has upset the best batsmen. ⏎ Watt has done well at times with his shorter run and slower deliveries. Harding has improved upon last year and next year, if there is cricket, he will be more deadly. Lewis greatly improved and when Wright was away in the Tests did wonderfully well. It is worth his while to practise batting. The other bowler to mention is J. W. Martin, the Catford amateur fast bowler. Given more practice in county cricket he should improve. ⏎ Thus we come to an end of the cricket season of 1939-a tragic end. Let us hope and pray that county cricket will be played again next season. |

| 82 | 2 | 1 | CAPTAIN NICHOLL'S-XI. v. MR J. A. BROOK'S XI. |
| 82 | 3 | 5 | A. Smith and H. Hodgson started for Carlisle after the luncheon interval; but Pye quickly disposed of the first named, clean bowling him for a "duck." With Jackson in, runs came freely, both men batting confidently. At 36 Goodband relieved Butler, and carried Hodgson's bails away with his first delivery. Downey and Todd quickly followed in, and out, but J. G. Hodgson and Jackson made another good stand. At length, in trying to pull one from Goodband, Jackson got his leg in front of the wicket, and was out. His 40 was a capital display of cricket, for he played well all round, being equally as strong on the leg side as on the off; and he drove well along the ground. When Jackson fell the total stood at 99, and with the "century" up Irwin followed him out. Immediately afterwards J. G. Wilson missed Hodgson; and for a time it looked as if this mistake would cost the Tynesiders the match, but at 110 Pye beat Stordy, and two runs later got rid of McIlvena. Then Goodband joined in, and getting Quirk and Brooks for "blobs," finished off the innings for 115, leaving the home team victorious by eight runs. There were about 1,000 people on the ground during the afternoon. Score:— |
