| 89 | 3 | 5 | Who is the very superior young man who writes on Northum- ⏎ berland cricket for ' P. I. '? Is he a player ? In his opinion there ⏎ is only one good team—the Borderers—in the county, and but ⏎ one competent critic of the game—himself. If rumour speaks ⏎ the truth, it is a well-known member of a prominent local club, ⏎ who some years ago came from the south with a great reputation ⏎ as a bowler; but with each succeeding season his reputation has ⏎ grown less, until now, not being able to score off his opponents on ⏎ the field with either bat or ball, he has taken to the pen, in the ⏎ hope, I suppose, of playing a winning game. Northumbrian ⏎ batsmen may not be Graces nor Stoddarts; but they are able to ⏎ hold their own with the bowlers which south have, up to now, ⏎ sent us, if we take the Borderers' "Knight of the pen" as a ⏎ specimen. |
| 89 | 4 | 6 | It is pure moonshine for any one to talk of the Borderers as re- ⏎ presenting the best cricketing talent in the county; and no ⏎ difficulty would be experienced in getting together a much ⏎ stronger eleven than they put into the field  In fact, what ⏎ victories they have gained here have been chiefly over school ⏎ boys, and were the best team chosen to play for Northumberland ⏎ probably only one Borderer—K Spencer—would be sure of a ⏎ place in it  At the present time several prominent county gentle- ⏎ men are trying to improve cricket in these parts. Towards this ⏎ end they are inviting strong southern teams to visit them; and ⏎ sarcastic remarks about those connected with the game, and the ⏎ players, from the pen of the Borderers' superior young man will ⏎ not help them much. Perhaps, after all, it is not county so much ⏎ as afternoon tea party cricket that this critic patronises, while ⏎ his own falling off in bowling he attributes to the weakness of ⏎ local batsmen, as he can only bowl well against good men. ⏎ What a pity he cannot play in the south. ⏎ Some time ago a committee was formed with the intention of ⏎ resuscitating county cricket in Northumberland; but as the ⏎ Corporation have refused to grant a field, and negotiations with ⏎ landowners for one have fallen through, Mr R. G. Mortimer ⏎ will, I believe, call a general meeting shortly, and lay the matter ⏎ before it. ⏎ I notice that the Elswick Works Club has fixed their match with ⏎ Benwell on July 27 as Lisey's benefit game. A more capable and ⏎ painstaking professional cannot be found than Lisey, and as a ⏎ player he ranks among the best in the north. His bowling has ⏎ pulled the club through many of their engagements, and I am ⏎ sure every member will do his best to increase the fund to be ⏎ handed over to their professional, who, by his ability on the field ⏎ and conduct off, does as much as any one connected with it to ⏎ uphold its honour and dignity. ⏎ I hope my readers will not forget that the game between ⏎ South Northumberland and Elswick Works, at Gosforth, on ⏎ Saturday, is for the benefit of the professional Goodband. This ⏎ player is certainly one of the best we have ever had in the North ⏎ of England, and deserves a bumper gate. ⏎ On Saturday Stockton visited Darlington, and, thanks to ⏎ McDonald's 75 not out, defeated the home team easily. Lieut. ⏎ Coates had another day out against Durham School, and had ⏎ hard lines in missing his century by 7 runs; while his batting ⏎ was followed up by some good bowling on the part of Fergu- ⏎ son. The matches between South Shields and Tynemouth for ⏎ Copeland's benefit, furnished some good cricket, but ended in a ⏎ draw. Wallsend could do little with the bowling of Simpson (5 ⏎ for 13) and Goodband (4 for 12), and were all out for 31. What ⏎ was the matter with North Durham on Saturday ? Did they all ⏎ get the "hump" together, for there was certainly something ⏎ wrong with them. For six of them to go down without scoring re- ⏎ quires some explanation. ⏎ Sunderland, though they succeeded in beating Consett at Con- ⏎ sett, could do no better than draw on Saturday at Sunderland; ⏎ though as the full five hours were not played I believe they ⏎ intend to claim the match. One of the best performances of the ⏎ day was that of Eley in the match against the Royal Grammar ⏎ School; for after taking seven wickets for 14 runs he came out ⏎ top scorer of his side with a well-played 28. Up to the beginning ⏎ of this month he had got 57 wickets for less than 6 runs each—a ⏎ bowling feat that will take some beating. ⏎ St. George's and Northumberland met on Saturday, in fine ⏎ weather, before a small gate. Northumberland batted first, and ⏎ were got rid of very cheaply for 113. Moss took five wickets; ⏎ Bell, three; and V. Thompson, two for 1 run. Hoare batted well ⏎ for 32. On St. George's going in a complete rot took place, and ⏎ they were all out in less than an hour for 38. Not the best form ⏎ was shown. Everyone seemed to think that because Thompson ⏎ got a duck there was no necessity for their getting any either. ⏎ This won't do. Their cracks cannot come off every time, and the ⏎ rest of the team must make up their minds to do a fair share in ⏎ the run getting. The fielding on both sides was very good. |

| 244 | 43 | 91 | 46 ⏎ CATHEDRAL GUILD v. MR. CONACHER'S XI.— ⏎ Played at Diglis, on Saturday, and resulted in a ⏎ decisive win for the Guild by 46 runs. The scratch ⏎ eleven, winning the toss, elected to bat, but, with the ⏎ exception of Mr. C. Riley, could make no stand ⏎ against the bowling of S. Smith and Phillips, the ⏎ former taking four wickets for 15, and the latter six ⏎ for 21. The total absence of extras proved that ⏎ Harford as the wickets was well up to his work. On ⏎ going to bat, the Guild fared badly, no one reaching ⏎ double figures till the eighth wicket was down, but ⏎ Walker, Darby, and Morgan put a different com- ⏎ plexion on the game, the captain playing extremely ⏎ well for his 22. Next Saturday the Guild play their ⏎ last fixture with St. John's at Diglis. Score:— ⏎ 	GUILD. Banks  4 ⏎ Extras ........ 31	Extras ........ 9 ⏎                    ---				     --- ⏎                    187				     222 ⏎  ⏎ Mr. R. B. Porch, late of Malvern College, ⏎ who plays for Somerset, is tenth in the batting averages ⏎ of that club with an average of 16.11. He has scored ⏎ 203 in 14 innings. ⏎  ⏎ St. John's, in their match on Thursday at Boughton, ⏎ against the Club and Ground, took a strong lead after ⏎ going in first. They made a capital stand, although ⏎ the bowling against them was rather warm. Burrows, ⏎ Willoughby, and Ryder shared between them what ⏎ wickets fell, before Mr. Young declared with nine ⏎ wickets down. Mr. H. V. Plum batted very steadily ⏎ for 48. Messrs. Curtis and Mansell also performed ⏎ creditably, and 123 was the total. This score looked ⏎ like being wiped off, for Burrows was in his smiting ⏎ humour and hit up 57 in lively fashion. The County ⏎ lot were only 13 behind and six wickets down when ⏎ time put a stop to proceedings. ⏎  ⏎ Mr. E. P. Gem bossed the team very well, and I ⏎ should think he has acted in a similar capacity before, ⏎ although I have not known him do so. ⏎  ⏎ It was rather funny that Straw should have been ⏎ objected to by the St. John's captain, because I have ⏎ an inkling that he has resorted to similar measures ⏎ before, viz., when he has been short of players he has ⏎ done the best he could to fill the vacancies. Although ⏎ he had a right to object to Straw, who had not fielded, ⏎ I think he might have given way, but I suppose it ⏎ was his customary zeal in trying to win the match that ⏎ made him forget previous circumstances. |
| 244 | 44 | 128 | The improved state of the ground struck me as soon ⏎ as I got there. I subsequently learned that the ⏎ brothers J. E. and E. Perkins, both of whom are ⏎ independent gentlemen, set to work and levelled 14,000 ⏎ feet with their own hands last winter. This sub- ⏎ stantiates what I have said of them before, that they ⏎ are the right men in the right place. This is ⏎ enthusiasm indeed, as those who have had a spell at ⏎ that sort of work know very well. The wicket was a ⏎ perfect one, and, after all the runs which were made, ⏎ was just as good at the close. |
| 244 | 45 | 129 | I had forgotten to say that there is about £45 yet ⏎ to be got to clear the pavilion debt, and I notice a match ⏎ will be played there between 22 ladies and 11 gentle- ⏎ men. This ought to wipe some of it off, for the ⏎ tickets are going off well. If the weather is fine there ⏎ will be a good gate and plenty of fun. |

C.C., LOWER ABBEY STREET.- ⏎ Special match practices Wednesday and Friday on ⏎ Public Cricket Ground. Members and friends please ⏎ attend.-H. Chandler, hon. sec., &c. ⏎ FIXTURES. ⏎ June 12-Leinster v. Athy. Rathmines. ⏎ June 12-Co. Clare v. St. Flannan's College. Claureen. ⏎ June 12—County Meath v. Pembroke. Navan. ⏎ June 13-County Meath v. Dublin Garrison. Navan. ⏎ June 13-Phoenix v. Athy. Phoenix ground. ⏎ June 13, 14, and 15-Dublin University v. Leicester- ⏎ ROYAL MUNSTER FUSILIERS V ARMY SERVICE CORPS. ⏎ BATTING EXTRAORDINARY-THE RECORD BEATEN. ⏎ Captain W. Caspe Oates, Royal Munster Fusiliers, ⏎ the popular Captain of the Curragh District C.C., and ⏎ Private Fitzgerald, of the same battalion, yesterday ⏎ had the distinction of topping the batting record by ⏎ compiling the immense score of 623 runs during their ⏎ partnership, and as both batsmen were not out at the ⏎ call of "time," when they seemed good for another 600, ⏎ it is impossible to estimate what might have happened ⏎ had the game been prolonged. The building up of this ⏎ huge score occupied exactly 4½ hours, yielding an ave- ⏎ rage of 131 runs per hour. 605 is supposed to have been ⏎ the previous record, that being the score credited to ⏎ Messrs A. H. Trevor and G. F. Vernon in a match, ⏎ Orleans Club v Rickley Green, some years ago, but it is ⏎ understood both batsmen were "out." Captain Oates, ⏎ who has done so much for cricket, especially for the ⏎ ground on which the immense score has been compiled, ⏎ richly merits the distinction of having contributed the ⏎ lion's share towards this remarkable performance, and ⏎ it bears eloquent testimony to the value of the improve- ⏎ ments to the ground inaugurated by him eight months ⏎ ago, that this new record score should have followed so ⏎ closely the score of over 400 runs, recently made by the ⏎ Curragh District against Dublin University. W. Frizell (in the ⏎ chair), Mr F. Stokes (Dundrum), Mr H. Thornton (Pem- ⏎ broke), Mr C. Mitchell (Pembroke), Mr J. Cotter (Dun- ⏎ drum), Mr F. H. Browning (Dublin University), hon. ⏎ sec. ; Mr J. Foxall (Leinster), hon. treasurer. Mr ⏎ Foxall announced that the receipts at the recent Gentle- ⏎ men of Ireland v M.C.C. match amounted to £52, and ⏎ after the discharge of all liabilities a balance of close on ⏎ £30 remained to the credit of the union-a very satis- ⏎ factory state of affairs. The Junior Cup competition ⏎ was discussed, and it was decided that clubs, in order ⏎ to be eligible, should be affiliated with the Leinster ⏎ branch by July 1st.
